SEBI Grade A Phase 2 – Paper 1 (2025)  
Essay: (250-270 words / 30 marks) (2025) 
Q1. Write an Essay of about 250 -270 words on any one of the following topics: (30 Marks) 
Q1. Write an Essay of about 250 -270 words on any one of the following topics: (30 Marks) 
1. Social Media and Elections: A Double-Edged Sword for Democracy 
The World Economic Forum has identified misinformation and disinformation among the top global risks 
in recent years, underscoring how digital platforms increasingly shape democratic discourse. Social media 
has transformed elections by enabling political parties, candidates and citizens to communicate instantly, 
but it has also amplified misinformation, polarization and algorithm-driven echo chambers. 
On the positive side, social media democratizes political participation. Platforms such as X, YouTube and 
Instagram allow leaders to engage directly with voters, particularly young citizens. According to the 
Election Commission of India, voter participation in recent elections has steadily improved, aided by digital 
awareness campaigns. Political outreach has become cheaper, faster and more inclusive, allowing regional 
voices to gain national visibility. 
However, these benefits come with significant risks. Recommendation algorithms often prioritize 
sensational content over verified information. Deepfakes, AI-generated images and coordinated 
misinformation campaigns can distort public opinion within hours. The World Economic Forum's Global 
Risks Report warns that manipulated information could undermine trust in democratic institutions. 
Excessive personalization also creates ideological "echo chambers," limiting informed debate and 
reinforcing confirmation bias. 
Recognizing these challenges, India has strengthened its regulatory ecosystem. The Election Commission of 
India issued social media guidelines for political parties, while digital intermediaries are expected to 
exercise greater due diligence under the Information Technology framework. Global initiatives such as the 
EU Digital Services Act also highlight the growing emphasis on platform accountability. 
As former UN Secretary-General Kofi Annan observed, "Knowledge is power. Information is liberating." Yet 
information becomes empowering only when it is credible. The future of democratic elections lies not in 
restricting digital expression but in promoting digital literacy, algorithmic transparency, responsible 
platform governance and ethical political communication. Social media should remain an instrument of 
democratic empowerment rather than democratic manipulation. 
 
2. Can Quick Commerce Become Sustainable? 
Quick commerce has transformed India's retail ecosystem by promising deliveries within 10–30 minutes. 
Driven by rapid urbanization, smartphone penetration and digital payments, the sector has witnessed 
remarkable growth. According to RedSeer Strategy Consultants, India's quick commerce market is 
expected to exceed $9 billion by 2029, reflecting strong consumer demand. However, questions regarding 
its long-term sustainability remain. 
The sector has generated substantial employment for delivery partners, strengthened local supply chains 
through dark stores, and enhanced consumer convenience. It has also accelerated the adoption of digital 
payments and inventory management technologies. Companies are increasingly leveraging artificial 
intelligence and predictive analytics to optimize inventory, reduce stock-outs and improve operational 
efficiency. 
Despite these gains, the business model faces significant challenges. Intense competition, high customer 
acquisition costs, discount-led growth and rising logistics expenses have put pressure on profitability. 
Environmental concerns also arise from excessive packaging waste, frequent vehicle trips and increased 
carbon emissions. Furthermore, the pressure to deliver within minutes often raises concerns about road 
safety and the welfare of gig workers. The NITI Aayog's report on India's Gig Economy highlights the need 
for stronger social security, insurance and welfare measures for platform workers. 

===== PAGE 235 =====
229 | P a g e  
Recognizing these concerns, companies are shifting from "growth at all costs" to sustainable expansion. 
Firms are expanding private labels, improving order density, adopting electric vehicles, promoting 
recyclable packaging and enhancing route optimization through artificial intelligence. The Economic Survey 
of India also emphasizes technology-led efficiency and green logistics as critical pillars for sustainable 
commerce. 
As management thinker Peter Drucker observed, "Efficiency is doing things right; effectiveness is doing the 
right things." The future of quick commerce lies not merely in faster deliveries but in building profitable, 
environmentally responsible and socially inclusive business models. Sustainable innovation, regulatory 
support and responsible corporate practices will determine whether quick commerce remains a temporary 
trend or becomes a lasting pillar of India's digital economy. 
 
3. Can High-Speed Rail Compete with Air Transport? 
Transportation is a critical driver of economic growth, regional integration and sustainable development. 
As countries invest in modern mobility infrastructure, high-speed rail (HSR) has emerged as a viable 
alternative to air travel, particularly for medium-distance routes. India's Mumbai–Ahmedabad High-Speed 
Rail Corridor, being developed with Japanese Shinkansen technology, marks a significant step in this 
direction. 
High-speed rail offers several advantages over aviation. It provides city-centre to city-centre connectivity, 
reducing total travel time by eliminating lengthy airport check-ins and transfers. According to the 
International Energy Agency (IEA), rail transport is among the most energy-efficient modes of passenger 
transport, producing substantially lower carbon emissions per passenger-kilometre than aviation. HSR also 
supports balanced regional development by connecting emerging cities with major economic hubs, thereby 
promoting investment, tourism and employment. 
However, aviation continues to enjoy distinct advantages. Airlines are indispensable for long-distance and 
international travel, where rail is neither practical nor cost-effective. The International Air Transport 
Association (IATA) projects that global air passenger traffic will continue to grow steadily over the coming 
decades, reflecting sustained demand. Moreover, developing HSR requires enormous capital investment, 
extensive land acquisition and long gestation periods, making financial viability a key challenge. 
Rather than viewing the two modes as competitors, policymakers increasingly advocate their integration. 
Countries such as Japan, France and China have successfully developed multimodal transport systems 
where high-speed rail complements aviation by serving short- and medium-haul routes, allowing airlines to 
focus on longer journeys. 
As Prime Minister Narendra Modi has stated, "Infrastructure is the foundation of a developed India." The 
future of transportation lies not in replacing air travel with high-speed rail but in creating an integrated, 
efficient and environmentally sustainable mobility network. A balanced transport ecosystem will enhance 
connectivity, reduce emissions and strengthen India's journey towards Viksit Bharat 2047. 
 
4. Indians in Global Leadership Positions: What Does It Mean for India? 
The growing presence of Indians in leadership positions across global institutions and multinational 
corporations reflects India's expanding economic, intellectual and diplomatic influence. From Ajay Banga 
leading the World Bank, Gita Gopinath serving as the First Deputy Managing Director of the International 
Monetary Fund (IMF), and Indra Nooyi and Sundar Pichai heading global corporations, Indian 
professionals are increasingly shaping global governance and business. 
This trend signifies India's emergence as a trusted source of skilled human capital. According to the World 
Economic Forum's Future of Jobs Report, India possesses one of the world's largest pools of digital and 
STEM talent. The country's demographic dividend, robust higher education institutions, thriving startup 
ecosystem and expanding digital economy have enabled Indian professionals to excel on the global stage. 
Global leadership by Indians also strengthens India's soft power. These leaders facilitate knowledge 

===== PAGE 236 =====
230 | P a g e  
transfer, encourage cross-border investments and enhance India's credibility in international policymaking. 
Their understanding of emerging markets enables them to advocate inclusive growth, digital public 
infrastructure and sustainable development. India's successful initiatives such as Aadhaar, Unified 
Payments Interface (UPI) and the Digital Public Infrastructure (DPI) framework are increasingly being 
studied and adopted by several countries. 
However, the phenomenon also highlights challenges. The migration of highly skilled professionals raises 
concerns regarding brain drain, while India's domestic institutions must continue creating world-class 
research opportunities, innovation ecosystems and leadership pathways to retain talent. The Economic 
Survey of India emphasizes that sustained investment in human capital is essential for achieving long-term 
economic growth. 
As former President Dr. A. P. J. Abdul Kalam observed, "Dream, dream, dream. Dreams transform into 
thoughts and thoughts result in action." The global success of Indians is not merely an individual 
achievement but a reflection of India's growing capabilities. By nurturing innovation, strengthening 
institutions and investing in human capital, India can convert global leadership into national development 
and reinforce its aspiration of becoming a leading global power by 2047. 
 
Q2. Make a Précis of the following passage in 140–160 words and give it a suitable title. (30 Marks) 
The announcement of a temporary trade arrangement between the United States and China—the world's 
two largest economies—has brought a measure of optimism to an otherwise uncertain global trade 
landscape. According to the agreement, the United States has reduced tariffs on Chinese imports from an 
exceptionally high 145% to 30% for a period of 90 days. In return, China has lowered its tariffs on American 
goods from 125% to 10%. Financial markets across the world welcomed this development enthusiastically, 
with major stock market indices recording gains ranging from 2% to nearly 4%, reflecting renewed investor 
confidence and expectations of reduced trade tensions. 
However, this agreement is not an isolated event but the outcome of several months of escalating 
economic confrontation. The latest phase of the tariff war began in February when U.S. President Donald 
Trump imposed fresh tariffs on imports from China, Mexico and Canada. Subsequently, in early April, the 
United States announced a temporary suspension of reciprocal tariffs on most countries for 90 days. China, 
however, was deliberately excluded from this relief, signalling Washington's intention to continue exerting 
maximum economic pressure on Beijing while keeping negotiations open. 
The temporary agreement has generated two contrasting interpretations among economists and policy 
analysts. One school of thought believes that the United States was compelled to soften its position 
because tariffs of 145% had become economically unsustainable. Such steep duties disrupted normal trade 
flows, increased costs for businesses and consumers, and ultimately weakened economic efficiency. The 
joint statement issued after the negotiations, which emphasized the significance of maintaining stable 
bilateral economic and trade relations, lends support to the argument that the economies of the United 
States and China remain deeply integrated and cannot be effectively separated through excessive tariff 
barriers. 
A different interpretation argues that the American strategy achieved its intended objective. According to 
this perspective, the extremely high tariffs were never designed to be permanent but were instead used as 
a negotiating instrument to compel China to engage in meaningful discussions. Since both countries 
eventually agreed to resume negotiations, supporters of this view believe the tariff pressure served its 
purpose. Nevertheless, the principal concern that triggered the trade dispute—the large and persistent 
trade deficit of the United States with China—has not yet been resolved. Although both governments have 
agreed to continue discussions, the long-term success of this temporary truce will depend upon the 
outcome of future negotiations once the 90-day period comes to an end. 
For India, the consequences of this development are both uncertain and significant. On one hand, an 
improvement in U.S.-China relations could redirect global investment flows back towards China, thereby 

===== PAGE 237 =====
231 | P a g e  
reducing the momentum of the China+1 diversification strategy that India has sought to leverage in recent 
years. Simultaneously, India continues to face unresolved trade issues with the United States, including the 
possibility of retaliatory action over tariffs imposed on steel and aluminium products. More importantly, 
India's widening trade deficit with China and its dependence on Chinese imports for critical industrial inputs 
and raw materials remain structural challenges. Unless both the Union and State governments undertake 
comprehensive labour, land and manufacturing reforms that enhance industrial competitiveness and 
facilitate large-scale production, India may fail to emerge as a major beneficiary of the ongoing 
reconfiguration of global supply chains despite the opportunities available. 
SOLUTION 
U.S.–China Trade Truce and Its Implications 
The temporary trade agreement between the United States and China has eased global trade tensions by 
significantly reducing tariffs for 90 days, leading to positive reactions in international financial markets. The 
agreement follows months of escalating tariff measures initiated by the United States, particularly against 
China. While one interpretation suggests that unsustainably high tariffs forced Washington to soften its 
stance, another argues that strong tariff pressure successfully brought China back to negotiations. Despite 
the temporary relief, the core issue of the U.S. trade deficit with China remains unresolved, and the 
continuation of talks will determine whether the truce endures. For India, the agreement presents both 
opportunities and challenges. Improved U.S.-China relations may weaken the China+1 strategy by 
redirecting investments toward China, while India's own trade tensions and structural dependence on 
Chinese imports highlight the urgent need for reforms to strengthen domestic manufacturing 
competitiveness. 
Q3. Reading Comprehension 
Instructions 
Read the passage given below and answer the questions based on the passage in your own words. (40 
Marks) 
The rapid expansion of the digital economy has transformed data centres into one of the most critical yet 
least visible components of modern infrastructure. Every online activity—whether cloud computing, 
artificial intelligence, digital banking, video streaming, e-commerce, or social networking—depends upon 
these facilities to store, process, and transmit enormous volumes of information. While users enjoy 
uninterrupted digital services with little awareness of the underlying infrastructure, an important 
environmental challenge often remains overlooked. Data centres consume vast quantities of freshwater, 
making them one of the fastest-growing industrial users of this limited natural resource. As the demand for 
digital technologies continues to increase globally, concerns regarding water security, environmental 
sustainability, and responsible resource management have become increasingly significant. 
The enormous computing power housed within data centres generates substantial heat because thousands 
of servers operate continuously throughout the day and night. If this heat is not effectively removed, 
servers may malfunction, resulting in equipment damage, service disruptions, and loss of valuable data. 
Consequently, cooling systems are indispensable to the functioning of every large data centre. Among the 
various cooling technologies available, evaporative cooling remains the most widely adopted due to its high 
efficiency and relatively lower operating costs. Under this method, large quantities of water circulate 
through cooling towers where the water absorbs heat generated by servers before evaporating into the 
surrounding atmosphere. Although technologically effective, this process consumes exceptionally large 
amounts of freshwater on a daily basis. 
The scale of water usage is remarkable. A single hyperscale data centre may require anywhere between 
one million and five million gallons of water every day merely to maintain optimal operating temperatures. 
This level of consumption is broadly comparable to the daily water needs of an entire medium-sized town. 
As digital services expand and the number of data centres continues to rise, their cumulative demand for 

===== PAGE 238 =====
232 | P a g e  
freshwater has become a growing concern among policymakers, environmental organisations, and 
sustainability experts. 
The increasing popularity of artificial intelligence has further intensified this challenge. Industry estimates 
suggest that data centres worldwide consumed nearly 660 billion litres of water during 2021 alone, and this 
figure is expected to rise substantially in the coming years as AI applications become more widespread. 
Training sophisticated large language models requires enormous computational resources operating 
continuously over extended periods. Such intensive computing generates additional heat, thereby 
increasing cooling requirements and further raising water consumption. Consequently, artificial intelligence 
has become an important factor contributing to the rapidly growing environmental footprint of the digital 
economy. 
The concentration of large technology companies—including Google, Microsoft, Meta, Amazon and other 
cloud service providers—has accelerated the construction of massive data centre campuses across North 
America, Europe and Asia. While these facilities enable digital innovation and economic growth, they 
simultaneously place unprecedented pressure on local freshwater resources. This growing dependence on 
water has attracted increasing scrutiny from governments, environmental researchers, civil society 
organisations, and local communities, all of whom question whether current patterns of digital expansion 
can remain environmentally sustainable over the long term. 
Despite these concerns, technology companies have increasingly acknowledged the need to reduce the 
environmental impact of their operations. Several organisations are investing heavily in research and 
innovation to develop cooling technologies that require significantly less water. One promising alternative 
is liquid cooling, where specially engineered coolants circulate directly around high-performance 
processors, removing heat far more efficiently than conventional air-based systems. Since this technology 
minimises dependence on evaporative cooling towers, it substantially lowers freshwater consumption 
while also improving energy efficiency. Another emerging solution is air cooling, which takes advantage of 
naturally cool climatic conditions to dissipate heat, reducing or even eliminating the need for water-
intensive cooling processes in suitable geographical locations. 
In addition to technological innovations, many companies are exploring the use of recycled, reclaimed, or 
treated wastewater instead of potable freshwater. Rather than drawing clean drinking water from rivers, 
lakes, or underground aquifers, these facilities utilise water that has already been treated for industrial 
purposes. Such measures help conserve freshwater resources while ensuring uninterrupted cooling 
operations. Several data centre operators have also committed themselves to becoming water positive, 
meaning they aim to replenish more water than they consume through rainwater harvesting, watershed 
restoration, groundwater recharge, and investments in local water conservation projects. 
Governments and regulatory authorities are likewise beginning to recognise the environmental 
implications of the rapidly expanding digital infrastructure. Policymakers in several countries are 
considering stricter environmental norms requiring data centres to disclose their water consumption, 
improve operational efficiency, and adopt sustainable cooling technologies. Environmental experts argue 
that transparent reporting of water usage should become as important as reporting greenhouse gas 
emissions, allowing regulators and the public to monitor the industry's ecological footprint more 
effectively. Such disclosure standards could encourage greater corporate accountability while promoting 
responsible management of scarce natural resources. 
The issue is particularly significant for countries experiencing recurring water scarcity. In regions where 
communities already struggle to secure adequate drinking water and irrigation supplies, the establishment 
of large data centres may intensify competition for limited freshwater resources. Without careful planning, 
industrial demand could place additional pressure on local ecosystems and reduce water availability for 
agriculture and households. Therefore, environmental impact assessments must consider not only energy 
consumption but also the long-term implications of water extraction before approving new projects. 
The challenge ultimately lies in balancing two equally important priorities. On one hand, data centres are 

===== PAGE 239 =====
233 | P a g e  
indispensable for supporting digital transformation, artificial intelligence, financial services, e-commerce, 
scientific research, and innovation-driven economic growth. On the other hand, unchecked expansion 
without sustainable resource management could threaten water security and environmental resilience. 
Achieving long-term sustainability will require coordinated efforts by governments, technology companies, 
researchers, and local communities. Through innovation, responsible regulation, transparent reporting, 
improved cooling technologies, and efficient water management practices, it is possible to ensure that the 
digital revolution continues without compromising one of humanity's most valuable natural resources—
freshwater. 
Q1. Why do data centres require such large quantities of water, and what role does evaporative 
cooling play in their operations? 
Answer - : Data centres generate enormous amounts of heat because thousands of servers operate 
continuously to process, store, and transmit digital information. Excessive heat can damage 
hardware, interrupt services, and lead to data loss, making efficient cooling indispensable. The most 
commonly used method is evaporative cooling, where water circulates through cooling towers, 
absorbs heat from servers, and evaporates into the atmosphere. Although this system is highly 
effective and cost-efficient, it consumes vast amounts of freshwater. Consequently, large data 
centres require millions of gallons of water daily, making them among the largest industrial 
consumers of freshwater resources. 
Q2. How has the rise of artificial intelligence intensified the water consumption crisis associated 
with data centres? 
Answer - : Artificial intelligence has significantly increased the demand for computing power, thereby 
intensifying the environmental footprint of data centres. Training advanced AI models requires 
powerful processors operating continuously for extended periods, generating substantial heat that 
necessitates additional cooling. Since most facilities rely on water-intensive evaporative cooling 
systems, higher computational workloads directly translate into greater freshwater consumption. As 
AI adoption expands across industries, global water demand from data centres is projected to rise 
sharply. This trend has raised concerns among policymakers and environmental experts regarding 
long-term water security, sustainable resource management, and the ecological consequences of 
rapid digital transformation. 
Q3. In what ways does the geographical placement of data centres worsen water stress in 
vulnerable regions? 
Answer - : Many data centres are established in locations where land is affordable and climatic 
conditions naturally reduce cooling costs. However, these regions often already experience water 
scarcity due to population growth, agricultural demand, and climate change. Consequently, industrial 
water extraction intensifies competition for limited freshwater resources, affecting local 
communities and ecosystems. In several areas, corporations and residents depend on the same 
groundwater reserves, leading to social and environmental concerns. Without careful site selection 
and sustainable planning, the expansion of data centres can deepen existing water inequalities and 
further strain regions already facing chronic water shortages. 
Q4. Explain the dual water dependency of data centres in relation to energy consumption. 
Answer - : Data centres depend on water in two interconnected ways. First, they consume 
substantial quantities directly through cooling systems that prevent servers from overheating. 
Second, they indirectly require water because the electricity powering these facilities is often 
generated by thermoelectric power plants, which themselves consume large volumes of water for 
cooling purposes. Thus, every increase in computing activity raises both electricity demand and 
freshwater usage simultaneously. This dual dependency significantly increases the overall 

===== PAGE 240 =====
234 | P a g e  
environmental footprint of the digital economy and highlights the need for integrated solutions that 
improve both energy efficiency and water conservation. 
Q5. What measures are being taken by governments and technology companies to reduce the 
water footprint of data centres, and are they sufficient? 
Answer - : Technology companies are investing in innovative cooling technologies, including liquid 
cooling, air-based systems, and the use of recycled or reclaimed water to reduce freshwater 
dependence. Many organisations have also pledged to become water-positive by replenishing more 
water than they consume through conservation initiatives. Governments, meanwhile, are introducing 
stricter environmental regulations, mandatory disclosure of water usage, and sustainability standards 
to improve transparency and accountability. Although these measures represent important progress, 
they remain insufficient given the rapid expansion of AI and cloud computing. Continued 
technological innovation, stronger regulation, and collaborative water management will be essential 
for long-term sustainability.

Q1. According to Accounting Standard (AS) 2 on Valuation of Inventories, which of the following 
are included within the definition of inventories? 
1. Goods held for sale in the ordinary course of business (Finished Goods). 
2. Goods that are in the process of production for such sale (Work-in-Progress). 
3. Raw materials, stores, consumables and supplies intended for use in the production of goods or 
rendering of services. 
4. Work-in-progress arising under construction contracts. 
A. 1, 3 and 4 only 
B. 2, 3 and 4 only 
C. 1, 2 and 4 only 
D. 1, 2 and 3 only 
E. 1, 2, 3 and 4 
Answer - D 
Q2. Which of the following expenditure is not included in the cost of Property, Plant and 
Equipment in accordance with Accounting Standard (AS) 10? 
A. Initial delivery and handling expenditure 
B. Expenses incurred for installation and assembly 
C. Site preparation costs 
D. Expenditure on advertising and promotional activities relating to the launch of a new product 
E. Professional fees directly attributable to installation 
Answer - D 
Q5. As per Accounting Standard (AS) 3 on Cash Flow Statements, insurance companies are required 
to prepare and present their cash flow statement using which of the following methods? 
A. Direct Method 
B. Accrual Method 
C. Fund Flow Method 
D. Both Direct and Indirect Methods 
E. Indirect Method 
Answer - A

1. Identification

B. 1--D, 2--C, 3--B, 4--A 
C. 1--C, 2--B, 3--D, 4--A

Q7. A company has work-in-progress inventory valued at ₹15,000 at the end of the current 
accounting year. To complete the production process, an additional expenditure of ₹10,000 will be 
incurred. The estimated selling price of the finished goods is ₹25,000, while brokerage expenses 
amount to 4% of the selling price. If the goods are not completed, their net realisable value will be 
nil. At what value should the inventory be recognised in the financial statements? 
A. ₹14,000 
B. ₹24,000 
C. ₹15,000 
D. ₹10,000 
E. ₹25,000 
Answer - A 
Q8. Which one of the following, if present, is most likely to reduce the reliability and neutrality of 
financial statements? 
A. Historical Cost Principle 
B. Prudence Principle 
C. Consistency Principle 
D. Personal Bias 
E. Accrual Concept 
Answer - D 
Q9. According to Accounting Standard (AS) 9 on Revenue Recognition, interest income should 
ordinarily be recognised on the basis of: 
A. Only after approval by the management 
B. On a cash receipt basis 
C. In proportion to the time period for which the amount remains outstanding, taking into account 
the applicable rate 
D. Only at the close of the accounting year 
E. Immediately on receipt of the amount 
Answer - C 
Q10. Under the revaluation model prescribed in Accounting Standard (AS) 10, revaluation of 
Property, Plant and Equipment should generally be carried out for: 
A. Only those assets that have been fully depreciated 
B. The entire class of Property, Plant and Equipment to which the asset belongs 
C. Only selected assets having a high market value 
D. A single individual asset whenever management considers it appropriate 
E. Assets that are proposed to be disposed of 
Answer - B

Q13. Two department heads adopt different approaches while supervising their teams. Manager X 
makes decisions independently, does not encourage employee participation and expects 
subordinates to strictly follow instructions. Manager Y, however, invites suggestions from team 
members, values their opinions and then takes the final decision after discussion. 
Which of the following correctly identifies the leadership styles adopted by Manager X and Manager 
Y, respectively? 
A. Democratic and Autocratic 
B. Bureaucratic and Laissez-faire 
C. Transformational and Transactional 
D. Autocratic and Democratic 
E. Laissez-faire and Bureaucratic 
Answer - D 
Q14. A commercial bank offers the following services to its customers: 
1. Basic Savings Bank Deposit Account (BSBDA) 
2. Premium credit card privileges 
3. Insurance protection covering life and accidental risks 
4. Dedicated relationship manager for personalised assistance 
Which of the following correctly matches these services with Maslow's hierarchy of needs in the same 
sequence? 
A. Physiological Needs, Esteem Needs, Safety Needs, Social Needs 
B. Safety Needs, Esteem Needs, Physiological Needs, Social Needs 
C. Physiological Needs, Social Needs, Safety Needs, Esteem Needs 
D. Esteem Needs, Physiological Needs, Social Needs, Safety Needs 
E. Social Needs, Safety Needs, Esteem Needs, Physiological Needs 
Answer - A

Q60. With reference to the RBI Master Direction -- Foreign Investment in India issued under FEMA, 
consider the following statements regarding foreign investment by NRIs and OCIs: 
1. Under the Foreign Exchange Management (Non-Debt Instruments) Rules, 2019 and the RBI Master 
Direction on Foreign Investment in India, an investment made by a NonResident Indian (NRI) on a 
non-repatriation basis in the capital instruments of an Indian company is treated as domestic 
investment. Such investment is therefore not counted towards sectoral caps on foreign investment 
and is treated at par with resident investments. 
2. As per the RBI Master Direction on Foreign Investment in India, an Overseas Citizen of India (OCI) 
can invest in Indian companies under the same terms as an NRI. When such investment is made on 
a non-repatriation basis, it is treated as domestic investment and therefore does not count towards 
the sectoral cap, subject to compliance with applicable entry routes and sectoral conditions. 
3. According to FEMA provisions and the RBI Master Direction on Foreign Investment in India, 
investments made by NRIs on a repatriation basis through NRE, FCNR(B), or inward remittance are 
treated as foreign investment. Such investments are counted toward the applicable sectoral caps 
and must comply with the entry route (automatic or government approval) prescribed for the 
sector. 
4. The RBI Master Direction on Foreign Investment in India provides that all investments by NRIs and 
OCIs, irrespective of whether they are made on a repatriation or nonrepatriation basis, are treated 
as domestic investments and therefore excluded from the calculation of sectoral caps on foreign 
investment in Indian companies. 
Which of the statements given above are correct? 
A. 1 and 2 only 
B. 1, 2 and 3 only 
C. 2, 3 and 4 only 
D. 1, 3 and 4 
E. 1, 2, 3 and 4 
Answer - A

Q62. Two companies, Umbrella Ltd. and Raincoat Ltd., have issued Certificates of Deposit (CDs). 
Their practices are as follows: Provision / Practice Umbrella Ltd. Raincoat Ltd. Minimum 
denomination Umbrella Ltd. issues Certificates of Deposit in denominations that are multiples of 
₹1 lakh. Raincoat Ltd. issues Certificates of Deposit in denominations that are multiples of ₹5 lakh. 
Minimum maturity Umbrella Ltd. issues Certificates of Deposit with a minimum maturity period of 
5 days. Raincoat Ltd. Issues Certificates of Deposit with a minimum maturity period of 6 months. 
Loan against CD Umbrella Ltd. allows loans to be granted against Certificates of Deposit issued by 
it. Raincoat Ltd. does not allow loans to be granted against Certificates of Depositissued by it. 

===== PAGE 255 =====
249 | P a g e  
Transferability Certificates of Deposit issued by Umbrella Ltd. Are freely transferable. Certificates 
of Deposit issued by Raincoat Ltd. are freely transferable. As per RBI guidelines on Certificates of 
Deposit, which provision is violated? 
A. Umbrella Ltd. violates denomination, maturity, and loan-against-CD norms 
B. Raincoat Ltd. violates denomination norm only 
C. Umbrella Ltd. violates only transferability norm 
D. Raincoat Ltd. violates maturity norm 
E. Both companies comply with all provision 
Answer - A
